Define:

Actually `strongly_normalizing` defined as `SN_intro : (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t` is exactly the accessibility predicate with a coinductive-like presentation? Actually it's an inductive with a positive occurrence in a function argument — it's like `Acc` but with the recursive occurrence in a strictly positive position. This is equivalent to well-foundedness of step. We can prove inversion and induction principles: `SN t` gives `forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'` by inversion. And we can do induction on SN? The induction principle for this inductive: `strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> P t) -> ...` Actually since the recursive occurrence is in the premise of the constructor only under a forall, the induction principle should be fine because the occurrence is positive (inside a function type from t' to Prop, i.e., strictly positive).

We can define `SN t := strongly_normalizing t` and prove closure properties.

For strong normalization proof using reducibility:

The standard locally nameless proof (like Software Foundations' Stlc / LN chapter, or the "locally nameless" tutorial by Charguéraud) uses:
- Substitution: `subst x u t` replacing free variable x with u; and lemmas about `open`/`subst` interactions, `lc_at` closure.
- Typing: weakening, substitution lemma for typing.
- LR: closure under `open`/`subst`.

Hmm, there might be an easier route: prove the logical relation using `strongly_normalizing` and its induction principle. The induction principle of `strongly_normalizing`:

```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```
Let me check how Coq generates it. The constructor `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t`. Recursive occurrence is `strongly_normalizing t'` inside a forall and an arrow — strictly positive. Induction principle: `P` holds for t if for all t, given (`forall t', step t t' -> P t'`) and (`forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'`) then P t. Yes.

Hmm, note this is like a "bar induction". We can also prove `strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t`, then use well-founded induction. Actually `Acc step t` unfolds to `forall t', step t t' -> Acc step t'` which is exactly the same shape, and `Acc` is the same as `strongly_normalizing`. There's a trivial proof by induction: `SN t -> Acc step t` by induction on SN (using SN_ind); and `Acc step t -> SN t` by induction on Acc. So they're isomorphic. We can just use `strongly_normalizing` as is; its induction principle may be fine.

Alternative approach: logical relation defined by *value* and *application* with explicit "step-closure" built into LR:

```
LR T t := SN t /\ (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> LRv T v)? 
```

Or simpler: by inversion on step, the reduct is either open body v (in the AppAbs case), or app t1' v (App1, impossible since abs doesn't step), or app (abs) t2' (App2 with v --> t2', impossible since value doesn't step). So t' = open body v. Then SN t'. Good — this avoids determinism as a general lemma; each case is by inversion.

More importantly, in the ST_AppAbs inversion we need the premise `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body)` to derive that the reduct is `open body v`. In the case where t' arises from ST_App1/ST_App2 we get contradictions. Note that ST_AppAbs also has premises; inversion gives them.

Let me look at this from the perspective of a known development. There's a well-known Coq proof of strong normalization for STLC (e.g., in the "Coq'Art" book or the "Girard's proof" tutorials). For CBV with a small-step relation requiring locally closed premises, the locally nameless setting adds complexity. Perhaps we can sidestep by strengthening the typing → lc lemma and having LR imply lc from the start.

### Part 1: Basic lemmas on lc_at and open/subst

Define:
```
Fixpoint subst_all (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...
Definition subst x u t := subst_all (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else tm_fvar y) t.
```
Actually maybe better to define `subst` directly as single-variable structural recursion, and a "subst_list"/valuation substitution as a function on fvars. For the fundamental theorem, we need substitution of a valuation for all free variables. Let's define `subst_all σ t` directly. Note under abstractions no shifting.

Free variables:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Definition occurs_free x t := In x (fv t).
```
Need lemmas:
- `fv_subst_all`: occurs_free x (subst_all σ t) -> (exists y, occurs_free y t /\ occurs_free x (σ y)) \/ ... Actually we need: if x ∉ FV(t) then subst_all σ t doesn't depend on σ x. And: if x ∉ FV(t), then x ∉ FV(σ?) hmm. Let's state what's needed:
  - `subst_all_fresh`: x ∉ FV(t) -> forall σ σ', (forall y, y ∈ FV(t) -> σ y = σ' y) -> subst_all σ t = subst_all σ' t.
  - `open_subst`: subst_all σ (open t u) = open (subst_all σ t) (subst_all σ u).
  - `fv_open`: FV (open t u) ⊆ FV(t) ∪ FV(u)? Actually since open replaces bvars and leaves fvars, FV(open t u) = FV(t) ∪ FV(u). Should hold exactly? For bvar indexes, open only touches bvars; fvars remain. Under abs, open_rec (S k) recurses. So yes FV(open t u) = FV(t) ∪ FV(u) as sets. But careful: bvar occurrences with index 0 replaced by u; fvars unchanged; so free vars of result = free vars of t plus free vars of u (if any bvar 0 occurs). If no bvar 0 occurs, u isn't inserted, but FV(open t u) ⊆ FV(t) ∪ FV(u) is what we need. Let's prove `forall x, x ∈ FV(open t u) -> x ∈ FV(t) ∨ x ∈ FV(u)`.
  - `lc_at` and `open`: if `lc_at k t` and `lc_at k u`... standard: opening a locally closed term with a locally closed term preserves closure. Specifically `lc_at k t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`.
  - `lc_open_inv`: if `lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`... inverse direction: if `lc_at k (open_rec k (fvar x) t)` and x fresh, then t is `lc_at (S k)`? Standard. Need for typing → lc of abs.

Let's think about what lc properties we actually need for the proof:
1. LR T t -> locally_closed t (for all T). Needed to construct steps? If our strategy avoids constructing steps... Let's see whether we can avoid constructing steps entirely.

Potential alternative definition avoiding step construction: Define LR as a *strong normalization predicate with a semantic structure* using multi-step:

`LR T t := SN t /\ ...`. For closure under steps, maybe we can use a different argument: instead of proving LR closed under single steps generally, prove the abs application-closure directly by induction on SN v, and handle ST_App2 by requiring LR T1 v' from LR T1 v — this closure seems necessary. To prove closure under steps of LR at arrow, we need to show `LR B (app t' v)` from LR B (app t v) and t --> t'. That is closure of LR B under the step app t v --> app t' v. That's a general "closure under expansion by steps in the first component of application" property. Hmm, this is circular but by induction on the type B, we can prove a general "LR closed under steps" property:

`LR_step : forall T t t', LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'.`

Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: need definition of LR Bool strong enough: SN t' from SN t; and the value-characteristic condition for t'. Wait, but does `multi_step` need the step in the right order? `multi_step : R x y -> multi R y z -> multi R x z`, so step t t' then multi t' t'' gives multi t t''. ✓.
  Alternatively define using step-closure directly with "all reducts of t that are values are canonical" and prove by induction that multi version follows. The multi version is easier for closure.
  
  Hmm, but do we even need the Bool characteristic condition at all? Let's see where it's used: In the fundamental theorem T_If case at type T, to prove LR T (if t1 t2 t3), we need:

Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_Bool : forall t, SN t -> LR Ty_Bool t
| LR_Arrow : forall A B t, SN t -> (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (tm_app t v)) -> LR (Ty_Arrow A B) t.

Alternative approach: **Use Girard/Tait reducibility with a "neutral" or "value" based definition** where LR is defined on *values* only and terms are handled via "all reduction paths lead to values in LR". Hmm.

Another very robust approach for deterministic CBV: define the logical relation on *normal forms / values* and prove via the "syntactic" approach: since step is deterministic, every term has at most one normal form; strong normalization = exists a value normal form? Let's reconsider the standard Tait-style proof for CBV STLC. In TAPL, the normalization proof is for *full beta reduction* (untyped-ish, but well-typed terms) and uses: `R_T(t)` defined as:
- R_Bool(t) = SN(t)
- R_{A→B}(t) = SN(t) ∧ ∀s, R_A(s) ⇒ R_B(t s)
and the fundamental theorem. For full beta, the abs case: need R_{A→B}(λx.body). Given R_B(subst x:=v body) for all v ∈ R_A... They prove the substitution lemma on R. The key difference: full beta has the rule `(λx.body) s --> body[s/x]` for arbitrary s, no value restriction. In CBV, the step rule requires value v. So the abs case only needs beta for values v, but the application closure needs to handle non-value arguments.

In TAPL's proof, for the abs case they need R_B (t s) for arbitrary s ∈ R_A; they use SN induction on s? They prove: if R_A(s) and R_B(body[s/x]) then R_B((λ.body) s). For full beta, (λ.body) s --> body[s/x], and SN of the app follows from SN of substr by expansion. For CBV, (λ.body) s does not beta-step when s is not a value; instead, it steps in s. But by SN induction on s, one can show SN(app (λ.body) s), and application closure R_B((λ.body) s w) similarly requires analyzing it. Hmm.

Wait — maybe define the arrow LR without app closure but directly with a "forall v value, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)" style for abstractions, and for non-abstraction terms use a different condition? Since we're constructing LR inductively, maybe we can define LR to be *closed under step* as a primitive requirement, plus "values of arrow type are functions whose applications to LR arguments are LR" and "values of Bool type are true/false":

```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_Bool : forall t, SN t -> (value t -> t = true \/ t = false) -> LR Bool t
| LR_Arrow : forall A B t, SN t -> (forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (open??))...
```
No, arrow values are abs terms; but a term of arrow type might be a non-value that reduces to an abs.

Standard CBV logical relation from Ahmed/Appel-style:

```
LR T t := SN t ∧ (value t → VR T t)   -- where VR is the value relation
VR Bool v := v = true ∨ v = false
VR (A→B) v := ∀ w, LR A w → LR B (v w)
```
This is a well-founded-ish definition: LR at arrow references LR (strictly smaller types) at the same type? `LR (A→B) v` uses `LR B (v w)` where B is a subterm type, and `LR A w`; no recursion on same type. `LR T t := SN t ∧ (value t → VR T t)` where VR (A→B) v := ∀w, LR A w → LR B (v w). This is a well-founded recursion on types, fine (structurally recursive on T). Then LR is closed under steps? Let's see: if LR T t and t --> t', then SN t' ✓. If value t', need VR T t'. For Bool: t' = true/false? Since t --> t', t is not a value (values don't step, assuming), so LR T t's value condition doesn't apply. We need something to conclude t' canonical. Hmm, again the same issue: a non-value t reducing to a value t' of Bool type, is t' necessarily true/false? For well-typed terms maybe, but semantically not implied.

Hmm. So the "closure under steps" of LR at base type requires knowing that reducts of well-typed terms have canonical values. This suggests defining LR at base type to include "all multi-step reducts that are values are canonical", which is closed under steps as shown.

Let's try this definition:

```
Definition LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ (forall t', t -->* t' -> value t' -> t' = tm_true \/ t' = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun t => SN t /\ (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
Wait, but in the arrow case, do we need the value condition? For the abs case we prove application closure; for other terms we prove closure via... Let's carefully test closure under steps:

`LR_step : LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'`.
- Bool: SN t' from SN t. For all t'', t' -->* t'' -> value t'' -> t'' = true/false: use multi_step with step t t'. ✓
- Arrow: SN t'. Need forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app t' v). We have LR B (app t v). Then `has_type_lc`: induction on typing derivation. T_Var: fvar x is lc_at 0 ✓.
T_App, T_If: straightforward.

LR definition (structurally recursive on T):
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=

  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> t' = tm_true \/ t' = tm_false)

Hmm, wait: do we need `locally_closed t` in LR? For LR_step at arrow we needed lc v to construct ST_App1. Yes. For LR at Bool maybe not, but including uniformly is fine if provable in each case.

Actually, do we need lc at Bool? LR_step at Bool doesn't need it, but uniformity is fine. Let's see if the fundamental theorem can prove `locally_closed (t·σ)` in each case:
- Var: σ x is LR T hence lc ✓ (valuation condition gives LR, which includes lc).

  So LR T v should imply locally_closed v (lc_at 0), hence lc_at k v. Good, and substitution preserves lc_at. Actually we need: `lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (σ x)) -> lc_at k (subst_all σ t)`.   But actually in the abs case, we don't need lc of the body from the IH; we have `has_type_lc` on the original typing derivation giving lc_at 1 t1, and substitution preserving it gives lc_at 1 (t1·σ). And to get lc of the abs, we can apply has_type_lc to `H x Hx` for the chosen fresh x. So we don't even need to reconstruct. 

- App case at type T2: need lc of `app (t1·σ) (t2·σ)`: lc of both from IHs (LR implies lc). ✓.
- True/False: lc trivial.
- If case: lc of the three substituted subterms from IHs. ✓.

SN conjunct:
- Var: SN (σ x) from LR.
- Abs: SN of abs always (no steps).
- App: LR B (app t1[σ] t2[σ]) — by definition of LR Arrow applied to LR (t1[σ]) and LR (t2[σ]) → LR B (app ...) → SN. ✓.
- True/False: SN of values.
- If at Bool: SN (if ...) by the if-SN lemma using SN of the three subterms.
- If at Arrow: need SN (if t1 t2 t3) ✓ same lemma, and application closure: for all v, LR A v -> LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v). Hmm, this is the problematic part as discussed. We need to prove application closure for if-terms.

Similarly, the Arrow conjunct in the Abs case: application closure for app (λ.body) v. And note the app term has type T2; if T2 is an arrow, LR T2 (app (λ.body) v) requires application closure again, recursively. So we need a general lemma allowing us to prove LR at any type for applications of "functions" to arguments.

Let's try to find a uniform way: Maybe define LR differently so that the application closure conjunct isn't needed for arbitrary terms; e.g., define LR as an inductive predicate generated by rules:

```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_Bool : forall t, lc t -> SN t -> (multi-reducts values canonical) -> LR Bool t
| LR_Arrow : forall A B t, lc t -> SN t -> (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app t v)) -> LR (A->B) t.
```
This is the same as the fixpoint. The difficulty is unchanged.

The fundamental issue: for an if-term at arrow type, LR requires application closure. Let's see if we can prove a general lemma:

**Lemma LR_app_if**: If `LR Bool t1`, `LR T t2`, `LR T t3`, and T is arrow (A→B), then for all v, LR A v -> LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v).

We know LR T t2 and LR T t3 give application closure: LR B (app t2 v), LR B (app t3 v).
We know SN t1. Do induction on SN t1 (with t2,t3,v fixed? but t1' varies and the induction hypothesis needs the same t2,t3,v):
P t1 := forall t2 t3, SN t2 -> SN t3 -> LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v).
Using SN_ind, IH: forall t1', step t1 t1' -> P t1'.
To prove P t1 for the if-term, we need `LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v)`. LR B includes lc, SN, and if B is arrow, application closure. Hmm, this is recursive on the type B. Wait, B is a subterm of T = A→B, and T is a subterm of the original type... In the fundamental theorem's if case, T is arbitrary. We would prove a lemma by induction on the type T: "LR closed under if" maybe. For T = Arrow A B, the application closure at B involves LR at B, a smaller type, which relates to a different lemma instance.

Maybe there's a smarter way: prove LR T t is *closed under arbitrary behaviors* via the concept of "t is strongly normalizing and all its reducts are ..."? Hmm.

Let's consider defining LR via a *Kripke-like* or "reduction-closed" formulation that makes T_If easy:

`LR T t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> Red T t')` where Red T t' is a "head" condition on irreducible/whnf? For CBV, we could use weak head normal forms.

Alternative: **Use the standard reducibility proof but with a different LR definition that avoids the application-closure conjunct for non-abs terms**, by defining LR on *values* and a separate "SN + would-be values" for terms. Let me look at how CBV logical relations are usually defined:

Standard CBV step-indexed / binary logical relation (Ahmed):
```
V⟦Bool⟧ = {true, false}
V⟦A→B⟧ = {λx.e | ∀v ∈ V⟦A⟧, e[v/x] ∈ E⟦B⟧}
E⟦T⟧ = {e | ∀e', e -->* e' value => e' ∈ V⟦T⟧}
```
Here E (expression relation) is defined via multi-step to values. This is a *coinductive-ish* definition but perfectly fine as a plain definition using the inductively defined multi-step! For strong normalization, we can define:
```
LR T t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> VR T t')
VR Bool v := v = true \/ v = false
VR (A→B) v := forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app v w)
```
Let's test this. Closure under steps: LR T t and t --> t'. lc t'? Does step preserve lc? We need a lemma: step preserves locally_closed. OK let me think about what is actually easiest to formalize in Coq for CBV. Perhaps the cleanest is to use a **Kripke-style / "all reducts" definition with explicit determinism and progress**, or maybe use a **big-step semantics** and prove equivalence? Let's consider yet another approach:

**Approach: Strong normalization via a well-founded measure from a logical relation defined by recursion on the type, where LR is "hereditarily SN under head reduction"**. Since step is deterministic, we can define the "result" of evaluating a term as a partial function; SN means the evaluation terminates. A cleaner concept: define `eval` relation (big-step) deterministically; prove that CBV small-step is equivalent to big-step; prove all well-typed closed terms evaluate to values by logical relations on big-step (which is much easier because beta substitution appears in the big-step rule and the LR can be defined on values); then SN follows because small-step SN ↔ big-step termination + progress.

Hmm, big-step logical relations for CBV type safety/normalization:
- `Eval t v`: t evaluates to value v.

I think the cleanest way is to prove a general "reduction closure" property of LR in both directions:
- LR_step (forward closure): LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'.
- LR_expand: t --> t' -> LR T t' -> LR T t? (backward) — This is NOT true in general: t = (λ.true) Ω? For STLC well-typed this doesn't happen but semantically, t could have multiple reducts, one of which is LR and another not. But if step is deterministic, t has at most one reduct, so backward closure holds: t --> t' and LR t' implies all reducts of t are LR? The only reduct is t' (deterministic), so SN t and value-reducts: if t -->* t'' value, then either t = t'' (but t steps, so no) or t'' is a reduct of t' (determinism) or... With determinism, t --> t' and t -->* t'' means t' -->* t''. So LR t' gives the value condition; SN t follows from SN t' + deterministic (need: if t's unique reduct is SN then t SN). So backward closure holds under determinism.

    - refl: app t1 t2 is a value? An app is never a value, so vacuous.

  Alternative: prove the multi-reduct condition via a different route: Show that for all t', if app t1 t2 -->* t' then LR B t' (stronger), by induction on the multi-step derivation, using LR_step (which is proven independently of the App case? LR_step uses the definition at each type, but it might be provable by induction on the type once we have the App lemma? Let's see: LR_step for type B uses LR B's definition and needs the App... hmm, LR_step for arrow type needs to construct step app t v --> app t' v and apply LR_step at B — mutual induction on types; at base type it's trivial (multi-reduct). So LR_step doesn't need the App fundamental lemma; it's a separate induction on types. Good. But to apply LR_step to `app t1 t2 --> s`, we need LR B (app t1 t2) — circular!

  So the multi-reduct condition needs a genuinely different argument. Standard approach: define LR for **non-arrow** base types via SN and for arrow via "SN ∧ application closure", and avoid the multi-reduct condition? Let's revisit.

Hmm OK. Let me think about what properties we truly need, and maybe define LR as an **inductive predicate generated by closure rules**, so that we prove membership only through constructors and avoid proving complex "all reducts" conditions:

Idea: Define LR T t inductively with rules:
- `LR_Bool t`: requires SN t and lc t and "value-reducts canonical"? Or maybe we can define LR T as a *strongly normalizing term all of whose reducts satisfy a syntactic form*, but generated inductively:

Actually here's a cleaner idea used in many formalizations: define `LR T t` as `SN t ∧ ...` where the "..." is defined by **structural recursion on the SN derivation**? That's not directly possible, but the "all multi-reducts" formulation can be replaced by a **step-indexed / SN-recursive** definition using the induction principle of SN. Hmm.

Let's look at the problem from a different angle: maybe prove SN directly by induction on typing with a cleverly chosen **inductive** logical predicate, similar to "hereditarily terminating" for CBV in the style of "Normalization by Evaluation" (typed closure conversion):

Define `LR T t` inductively:
```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_Bool_true : LR Bool true
| LR_Bool_false : LR Bool false
| LR_Arrow : forall A B body, lc (abs A body) -> (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) -> LR (A→B) (abs A body)
| LR_neutral : forall T t, SN t -> (forall t', step t t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t   (* non-value step closure *)
```
Hmm, this is like defining LR as the least set closed under: values of canonical form, and terms all of whose one-step reducts are LR (plus SN?). Since step is deterministic-ish, this might work! Let's think.

For CBV:
- Values: true, false, abs.
  - LR Bool true := True; LR Bool false := True.
  - LR (A→B) (abs A body) := locally_closed (abs A body) ∧ forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v).
- Non-value terms: LR T t if all one-step reducts are LR T (and maybe SN t? but if all reducts are LR, is t SN? LR should imply SN; induction on the derivation could establish it if each rule preserves SN. For the non-value rule, if all reducts are LR hence SN, then SN t by SN_intro? SN_intro requires all reducts SN, which we get from IH (LR → SN). But this is an inductive definition, so the "all reducts" branching is fine for an inductive predicate (positive occurrence under forall).
But is this inductive definition well-founded? A non-value term t with a self-loop step t --> t would be excluded by SN. The rule "all reducts LR ⇒ LR t" would allow t if t's only reduct is t itself? No: to prove LR t via that rule, we need LR t for the reduct t, which is circular; the inductive definition requires a finite derivation, so t itself needs a smaller derivation. Since SN is well-founded, every SN term has a finite "derivation" by induction on SN. And for non-SN terms, no finite derivation exists? Hmm, coinductive-style loops: t with t --> t would need LR t to prove LR t — no finite proof, so not in the inductive set. But what about a term t that steps to u, u steps to t? No finite proof either. So the inductive LR should imply SN. Sounds promising!

Let's see whether this inductive definition makes the proof manageable.

Definition (inductive):
```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_true : LR Ty_Bool tm_true
| LR_false : LR Ty_Bool tm_false
| LR_abs : forall A B body,
    locally_closed (tm_abs A body) ->
    (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) ->
    LR (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs A body)
| LR_step : forall T t,
    (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ->      (* all reducts LR *)
    LR T t.
```
Wait, the last rule would make LR_t of any term whose all reducts are LR. But note: true has no reducts, so LR_step would also derive LR Bool true. That's fine (redundant). But does this definition imply SN? Prove `LR T t -> SN t` by induction on the LR derivation:
- LR_true: SN true (no steps).
- LR_false: SN false.
- LR_abs: SN abs (no steps).
- LR_step: Given forall t', step t t' -> LR T t' and IH: forall t', step t t' -> SN t'. Then SN_intro. ✓
Great, so LR implies SN easily, without needing lc in LR except for the abs rule (needed for the step relation's ST_AppAbs premise when beta-reducing app (abs body) v).

Does LR step-closure hold forward? If LR T t and t --> t', is LR T t'? By induction on the derivation of LR T t:
- LR_true/LR_false/LR_abs: values don't step, contradiction (need value_no_step lemmas).
- LR_step: the hypothesis gives LR T t' directly. ✓
So LR_step: LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'. But wait, in the LR_abs case, we need "abs doesn't step". Yes.
Backward expansion: t --> t' and LR T t' -> LR T t? Doesn't hold in general because other reducts of t might not be LR (nondeterminism). But step is deterministic; if t --> t' and t --> t'' then t' = t''. So we can prove LR t via LR_step if all reducts are LR: given t --> t'', determinism gives t'' = t', then LR t'. So backward closure needs determinism. Maybe we can avoid needing it, but it's provable.

Now, does this LR support the fundamental theorem? Let's check each case. But first: does the abs case's beta condition work nicely? Yes! Because the abs case's LR rule wants `LR B (open body' v)` for body' = body·σ. And substitution commutes with open.

  - ST_AppAbs: t1 = tm_abs T body (and the step premise gives lc (abs T body), value t2). Then we need LR B (open body t2). Does LR (A→B) (abs T body) give us the LR_abs condition? Not necessarily! We could remove the LR_step rule for terms that are values, or define LR such that the rules are:
```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_true : LR Bool true
| LR_false : LR Bool false
| LR_abs : forall A B body, lc (abs A body) -> (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) -> LR (A→B) (abs A body)
| LR_step : forall T t, ~ value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t
```
Adding `~ value t` to the step rule ensures that if t is a value, only the value rules apply. Then LR (A→B) (abs body) must be derived by LR_abs, giving the condition. But then for `LR Bool true`, only LR_true applies (assuming true is a value, so LR_step can't). Good. But what about a term like `if true then ... else ...` that steps? It's not a value, so LR_step applies. Good.

Then prove properties by induction on the derivation, with matching cases.

But there's still an issue: in the LR_step case of LR_app, t1 is not a value. Great, this inductive LR with the `~ value` side condition on the step rule seems promising!

Now the App case with LR_app as discussed. Also we need to check **LR_SN** (LR implies SN) and **LR_step** (forward closure). Let's verify LR_SN by induction on the LR derivation:

Then SN_intro t with proof: for all t', step t t' -> SN t' — from IH (each step t t' gives LR T t' by Hall and SN t' by IH on that LR derivation). Wait, IH is `forall t', step t t' -> LR T t' -> SN t'`? No: the induction is on the derivation of LR T t; the IH for premise Hall's *use* — but Hall is a hypothesis of the rule, not a derivation. The inductive definition's induction principle: for the LR_step constructor with argument Hall : forall t', step t t' -> LR T t', the IH would be `forall t', step t t' -> (LR T t' -> ...)`? Actually since Hall's type contains LR T t' in a negative position? Let me think: the constructor is
  `LR_step : forall T t, ~ value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t`.
The recursive occurrence `LR T t'` appears in the argument of a forall/arrow, i.e., in a *strictly positive* position? The constructor type is `... -> (forall t', step t t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t`. The occurrence of LR T t' is to the right of arrows inside the argument type — strictly positive. Coq's induction principle for this inductive should generate an IH:
  `forall T t (Hnv : ~ value t) (Hall : forall t', step t t' -> LR T t'), (forall t' (Hs : step t t'), P T t') -> P T t`?
Hmm, more precisely, the induction principle generated for a constructor whose argument is a function type containing recursive occurrences gives the IH as a function: `(forall t' (Hs : step t t'), P T t')`. This is because the induction principle treats each recursive occurrence; in a function argument `forall t', step t t' -> LR T t'`, the recursive occurrence is in the codomain, yielding IH `forall t', step t t' -> P t'`. Yes, Coq generates that.

So for LR_SN with P T t := SN t, the LR_step case gives Hall and IH : forall t', step t t' -> SN t', and we conclude SN t via SN_intro t Hall' where Hall' = IH. Wait, SN_intro requires `forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'`, which is exactly IH. So SN t. ✓ And we didn't even need Hall! Nice.

- LR_abs case: SN (abs). ✓ via no step.

LR_step (forward closure): P T t := forall t', step t t' -> LR T t'. By induction on LR derivation:
- LR_true: derive LR Bool t' from step true t' — contradiction with value_no_step (true is a value).
- LR_false: same.
- LR_abs: contradiction (abs is a value).
- LR_step: Hall : forall t'', step t t'' -> LR T t''. Need for all t', step t t' -> LR T t' — that's Hall directly. ✓
Great.

LR_app proof: as discussed, by outer SN_ind on SN t1, inner SN_ind on SN t2. But note: within the outer induction, we need SN t1 from LR (A→B) t1, which we have via LR_SN. The statement:
```
Lemma LR_app : forall A B t1 t2, LR (Ty_Arrow A B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR (Ty_Arrow A B ... ) 
```
Actually conclusion LR B (tm_app t1 t2). Proof:
```
intros A B t1. remember (SN (t1))? 
```
Let's do:
```
Lemma LR_app_aux : forall A B t1, SN t1 -> forall t2, LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros A B t1 Hsn. induction Hsn as [t1 Hstep IH]? 
```
The induction principle of `strongly_normalizing`: `strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t`. Let me check the exact form by testing in Coq. Since the constructor is `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t`, the induction principle should be:
`forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t`.
Note the second hypothesis is the actual constructor argument. So when we do `induction Hsn`, we get for the case: goal P t1 with hypotheses `IH : forall t', step t1 t' -> P t'` and `H : forall t', step t1 t' -> SN t'`. Hmm, is the order such that the IH is available? Let's verify in Coq later.

Set P t1 := forall t2, LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2). Wait, A B are fixed by the lemma; but the induction needs them universally quantified? If A B are fixed before induction, fine: prove for fixed A,B. In the LR_step case of t1's LR derivation, we need to apply IH (on SN) to t1' — but the IH from outer SN_ind has P t1' which is for all t2, LR (A→B) t1' -> LR A t2 -> ... — same A B, fine.

Now to prove P t1 given IH_outer and HstepSN, we need to analyze LR (A→B) t1. Do `remember (Ty_Arrow A B) as T eqn:...`? Better: `intros t2 HLR1 HLR2.` Then we need LR B (app t1 t2). We could do induction on HLR1 (the LR derivation), but we have IH_outer for reducts of t1. Let's do case analysis on HLR1:
- LR_abs case: t1 = tm_abs A0 body with A0 = A? Wait, t1 : LR (Ty_Arrow A B); the LR_abs constructor requires the type to be Ty_Arrow A' B' and t1 = tm_abs A' body. Since the type is literally Ty_Arrow A B, unification gives A' = A, B' = B. Good. Hypotheses: Hlc : locally_closed (abs A body), Hbody : forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v). Goal LR B (app (abs A body) t2).
  Use LR_step: ~value(app ...) ✓; for all t', step (app (abs body) t2) t' -> LR B t'. Invert step:
   * ST_AppAbs: t' = open body v with t2 = v (and premises). Need LR B (open body t2): Hbody t2 HLR2 ✓.
   * ST_App1: step (abs A body) t1' — impossible via value_no_step (abs is a value since Hlc).
   * ST_App2: value (abs A body), step t2 t2', t' = app (abs A body) t2'. Need LR B (app (abs A body) t2'). Use inner induction on SN t2 with statement Q t2 := LR A t2 -> LR B (app (abs A body) t2)? Hmm, but the inner induction would be over t2 with the *fixed* abs; we need it for t2'. Let's formulate a separate inner lemma:
     ```
     Lemma LR_app_abs : forall A B body, locally_closed (abs A body) -> (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) -> forall t2, SN t2 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app (abs A body) t2).
     ```
     Prove by inner SN_ind on SN t2: Given t2, IH: forall t2', step t2 t2' -> Q t2', and step-SN hypothesis. Need LR B (app (abs body) t2): use LR_step; reducts via inversion:
       ST_AppAbs: t' = open body v with t2 = v value: Hbody v HLR2 ✓.
       ST_App1: abs steps — impossible.
       ST_App2: t2 --> t2', t' = app (abs body) t2': use IH with step t2 t2', plus LR A t2' from LR_step. ✓
     Note the LR_step rule requires ~value(app ...) ✓.
     Great. Then the outer LR_abs case uses this inner lemma with HLR2 (gives SN t2 via LR_SN) and HLR2.
- LR_step case: t1 not a value, Hall : forall t1', step t1 t1' -> LR (A→B) t1'. Goal LR B (app t1 t2). Use LR_step: need ~value(app ...) ✓ (apps never values); for all t', step (app t1 t2) t' -> LR B t':
   * ST_AppAbs: would give t1 = abs ..., hence value t1 (using lc premise), contradicting Hnv.
   * ST_App1: t1 --> t1', t' = app t1' t2. Apply IH_outer t1' Hs (which gives P t1') with Hall t1' Hs and HLR2. ✓
   * ST_App2: requires value t1, contradiction Hnv.
  ✓.

- Wait, are there other LR constructors? LR_true and LR_false have type Bool, impossible since the type is Arrow A B. So the cases are LR_abs and LR_step.

So LR_app is proven by combining outer SN induction on t1 and an inner lemma.

Hmm, actually can we simplify: prove a general lemma by induction on the LR derivation of t1? The issue was LR_step cases handle reducts; but the LR_abs case works, and LR_step case works with IH_outer on SN t1 (not the LR derivation). We need SN t1 anyway. Let me structure as above.

Alternatively, prove LR_app by outer SN induction on t1 with a case analysis on LR t1 derivation; in the LR_abs case call the inner lemma.

Now the substitution interpretation details. Let's define everything carefully.

### Definitions and lemmas needed

**Substitution.**
```
Fixpoint subst_all (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all σ t1) (subst_all σ t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_all σ t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_all σ t1) (subst_all σ t2) (subst_all σ t3)
  end.
```
Free variables:
```
Fixpoint fvs (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvs t1 ++ fvs t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvs t1
  | tm_true | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fvs t1 ++ fvs t2 ++ fvs t3
  end.
Definition fresh_for (t : tm) (L : list atom) : atom := ...
```
We need an existence lemma: for any list L, exists x, ~ In x L. Use a standard construction: `fresh (L : list atom) := fold_right (fun x acc => max (S x) acc) 0 L`? Simpler: there's `Atom` in Metalib but we don't have it. We can define `max_list` or use the fact that nat is infinite: given a list, choose `S (fold_right max 0 L)`.

```
Fixpoint max_list (L : list nat) : nat :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => max x (max_list xs)
  end.
Lemma max_list_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (max_list L).
Lemma fresh_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
```

We also need freshness for variables not free in a term:
```
Lemma fresh_fvs : forall t L, ~ In (fresh (L ++ fvs t)) (L ++ fvs t).
```
We'll choose x = fresh (L ++ fvs t) so that x ∉ L and x ∉ fvs t.

**Key substitution/open lemmas.**
- `subst_all_open_rec : forall σ k u t, subst_all σ (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst_all σ u) (subst_all σ t)`.
  Prove by induction on t (generalizing k u). Note: `open_rec` uses `Nat.eqb k i`; substitution doesn't affect it.
- `subst_all_lc_at : forall σ k t, lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (σ x)) -> lc_at k (subst_all σ t)`.
  Actually since σ images are locally_closed values (lc_at 0), and lc_at 0 → lc_at k (monotone), we can state: lc_at k t -> (forall x, locally_closed (σ x)) -> lc_at k (subst_all σ t). Prove by induction on t generalizing k. Under abs, k increases; lc_at (S k) body from lc_at k (abs); σ images closed → lc_at (S k) via monotonicity. Need monotonicity lemma.
- `subst_all_id_on_fvs`? For the valuation extension: if x ∉ fvs t, then subst_all σ' t = subst_all σ t when σ' agrees with σ on fvs t and σ' x = v. Better: define σ' as `update_subst σ x v := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else σ y`. Then we need:
  - If x ∉ fvs t, then subst_all (update_subst σ x v) t = subst_all σ t. Prove via a general lemma: `subst_all_ext : (forall y, In y (fvs t) -> σ y = τ y) -> subst_all σ t = subst_all τ t`. Under abs, fvs body unchanged; okay. Under bvar, trivial. Need `if Nat.eqb x y` with x ∉ fvs t and y ∈ fvs t → x ≠ y → Nat.eqb x y = false. Use `Nat.eqb_eq`/`Nat.eqb_neq`.
  - `subst_all (update_subst σ x v) (tm_fvar x) = v` (Nat.eqb x x = true).
- The T_Abs commutation:
  Let body' = subst_all σ t1. We want: subst_all (update_subst σ x v) (open t1 (fvar x)) = open body' v, given x ∉ fvs t1.
  Proof: rewrite subst_all_open_rec with σ' := update_subst σ x v:
    subst_all σ' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst_all σ' t1) (subst_all σ' (fvar x))
    = open (subst_all σ' t1) v.
    And subst_all σ' t1 = subst_all σ t1 since x ∉ fvs t1. ✓
  Also open_rec at k=0; `open t1 u = open_rec 0 u t1`. ✓

- `lc_at` inversion/opening lemmas:
  - `lc_at_mono : lc_at k t -> k <= k' -> lc_at k' t`. Induction on lc_at? Or induction on t. The lc_at is an inductive predicate; proving by induction on it: cases have i < k; need i < k'. ✓ with `Nat.lt_le_trans` or lia. Abs case: lc_at (S k) body, need lc_at (S k') body; apply IH with S k <= S k'. ✓. App/if trivial.
  - `lc_at_open_rec : lc_at k t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`. Induction on t generalizing k? Let's do induction on t; need to handle bvar case: open_rec k u (bvar i) = if Nat.eqb k i then u else bvar i. If k=i: need lc_at k u ✓. Else: lc_at k (bvar i) gives i < k. ✓. Abs case: lc_at k (abs T t1) means lc_at (S k) t1; IH with k := S k and u: lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u t1); need lc_at k (abs T (open_rec (S k) u t1)) — by lc_abs. But the hypothesis is lc_at k (abs T t1), so inversion gives lc_at (S k) t1. We also need lc_at (S k) u from lc_at k u by monotonicity. ✓.
  - `lc_at_open_inv : lc_at k (open_rec k u t) -> lc_at k u -> lc_at (S k) t`? Hmm, wait: earlier I argued this. Let's double check the index handling: lc_at (S k) t means bvars < k+1. open_rec k u t replaces bvar k. Result's bvars: bvars of t other than k, plus bvars of u. If result lc_at k: bvars of t that are < k remain < k ≤ k+1 ✓; bvar k replaced; bvars > k would remain > k, violating lc_at k, so none. So lc_at (S k) t ✓. Prove by induction on t generalizing k.
    - bvar i: open_rec k u (bvar i) = if k=i then u else bvar i. Suppose lc_at k (result) and lc_at k u. Need lc_at (S k) (bvar i): need i < S k, i.e., i = k or i < k. If k=i ✓. Else result = bvar i and lc_at k (bvar i) gives i < k ✓.
    - fvar: lc_fvar ✓.
    - abs: result = abs T (open_rec (S k) u t1), lc_at k result → lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u t1). Need lc_at (S k) u: from lc_at k u by monotone. Apply IH (with k := S k) to get lc_at (S (S k)) t1. Then lc_abs gives lc_at (S k) (abs T t1) ✓.
    - app, if: straightforward.
  In `has_type_lc` T_Abs: from lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) and lc_at 0 (fvar x), get lc_at 1 t1. ✓.

- `has_type_lc : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> locally_closed t`. Induction on the derivation:
  - T_Var: lc_fvar ✓.
  - T_Abs: choose x fresh from L, get premise, IH gives lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x)); lc_at_open_inv with u = fvar x (lc_fvar) gives lc_at 1 t1; then lc_abs. ✓
  - T_App, T_True, T_False, T_If: ✓.
  Note: the IH is on the subderivation; but in T_Abs the subderivation is `H x Hx` for the chosen x. Fine.

- `value_no_step : value v -> step v v' -> False`. Induction on value? Cases: v_abs: step (tm_abs T t) v' — inversion on step: all constructors have LHS with app or if, so no match. We can prove `abs_no_step : step (tm_abs T t) v' -> False` and `step_true_false` similarly by `intro H; inversion H` (all constructors' conclusions are apps/ifs; Coq's inversion should solve). Let's check: step constructors: ST_AppAbs: tm_app ... --> ...; ST_App1, ST_App2: app; ST_IfTrue, ST_IfFalse, ST_If: if. So `step (tm_abs T t) v'` is impossible by inversion (no constructor unifies). Yes, `inversion H` should close the goal. Similarly for true/false.

**LR definition details.** Let's write:
```
Inductive LR : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| LR_true : LR Ty_Bool tm_true
| LR_false : LR Ty_Bool tm_false
| LR_abs : forall A B body,
    locally_closed (tm_abs A body) ->
    (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) ->
    LR (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs A body)
| LR_step : forall T t,
    ~ value t ->
    (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ->
    LR T t.
```
Wait, do we need `~ value t` in LR_step? Without it, LR could be derived for values via vacuous step. With it, we ensure canonical value rules. But note: a value of Bool type other than true/false? Values are abs/true/false; abs has arrow type only in LR_abs. True/false at arrow type can't be LR anymore (LR_step excluded since they're values). Is that a problem? Consider a well-typed term of arrow type that evaluates to... true? Impossible by typing, semantically. But could LR at arrow type be needed for true in some proof? In the fundamental theorem, t·σ has the right type; true only arises at Bool type. So no.

Hmm, but wait: is LR_step's `~ value t` going to cause trouble in the LR_app proof's non-value case? We had `Hnv : ~ value t1` from LR_step inversion; fine. And for the LR_abs case, fine.

But now, do we actually need LR to imply `locally_closed`? For the LR_abs rule, we included lc of the abs as a premise (needed for ST_AppAbs in LR_app). Where do we get lc of `abs A (body·σ)`? From has_type_lc + subst preserves lc. Fine.

Do we need lc for LR_step terms? For LR_app with non-value t1: the reduct app t1' t2 via ST_App1 needs lc t2 as a premise, but we only *invert* given steps, not construct them... Wait, in the LR_step case of LR_app, we prove LR B (app t1 t2) via the LR_step rule: need for all t' with step (app t1 t2) t', LR B t'. We invert the given step; no construction. ✓. In the ST_App2 branch of LR_app_abs, we use the inner IH — no construction. So we never construct steps! Let me double check: LR_step rule requires providing a proof of `forall t', app t1 t2 --> t' -> LR B t'`. We get t' and a proof of step; we invert. So no lc needed except in the LR_abs rule premise (lc (abs)), which is needed when the given step is ST_AppAbs? No wait — the ST_AppAbs inversion gives us the lc premise *from the step derivation*, so we don't need our own lc for that. Hmm, then why does LR_abs need lc at all?

Let me check where lc (abs A body) is needed in LR_app's LR_abs case:
- ST_AppAbs branch: given step (app (abs A body) t2) t'. Inversion gives t2 = v and value v and t' = open body v; the lc premise of the step constructor is about the SAME abs (tm_abs A body) so it's given by the step derivation. We don't need Hlc.
- ST_App1: need to derive contradiction: step (abs A body) t1'. This is impossible regardless of lc (abs never steps). We don't need lc.
- ST_App2: requires `value (tm_abs A body)` as a premise of the step constructor. To derive a contradiction? No, ST_App2 is possible: value (abs A body) requires locally_closed (abs A body)! So if lc doesn't hold, ST_App2 can't fire either. Either way, we handle ST_App2 by the inner lemma using t2 --> t2'; we don't need value t1. Actually if ST_App2 fired, we get the premise `value (tm_abs A body)` from inversion, which includes lc, but we don't need it for the branch result: we need LR B (app (abs A body) t2'), which the inner lemma proves using Hbody and LR A t2'. Hbody doesn't need lc. Does the inner lemma need lc? It uses LR_step for app (abs body) t2; in the ST_AppAbs branch, inversion gives what we need; ST_App1 impossible; ST_App2 handled by IH. So no lc needed! Interesting. And the inner lemma's ST_AppAbs branch: inversion gives t' = open body v where t2 = v and value v; Hbody t2 HLR2 gives LR B (open body t2) ✓. Great.
  
So maybe LR_abs doesn't need the lc premise at all! Let's double check that the "SN of abs" lemma works without lc: abs never steps, so SN regardless. ✓. And LR_step forward closure: LR_abs case: t1 = abs, step abs t' impossible regardless of lc ✓. So we can drop lc from LR entirely. That simplifies the fundamental theorem's abs case enormously (no need for has_type_lc, no substitution-lc lemmas)! Let me re-examine LR_app_abs: it needs only `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)` and t2's LR/SN. Great.

Wait, but is there a subtlety: LR_abs as a rule for `tm_abs A body` — should we require lc? Consider LR (A→B) (abs A (body)) where body has a dangling bvar. Semantically weird but our proofs may not care. The LR_SN gives SN (abs) which holds regardless. The beta condition talks about open body v. In the fundamental theorem, the body is well-typed so fine. Dropping lc is acceptable if all proofs go through. Since we never construct steps, lc is indeed unnecessary. Nice, this removes the whole has_type_lc development. But wait: in the ST_App2 inversion for LR_app, the premise `value (tm_abs A body)` might be needed to know that ST_App2's reduct is `app (abs A body) t2'` — that's just the shape, no lc needed. ✓.

However, hold on: do we need `~ value t` in LR_step? With LR_abs not requiring lc, the value predicate for abs requires lc. Consider a term t that is `tm_abs A body` with body not lc (so not a value). LR_step with ~value t could apply to it. That's fine. The point of ~value t is to ensure that if t IS a value (true/false/lc-abs), then LR gives canonical structure. For true: if LR Bool true via LR_step? But true is a value, so excluded. Good. For abs: an lc abs is a value; LR via LR_abs gives beta condition. An abs that is not lc is not a value; LR_step could apply, and then it has no reducts so Hall is vacuous, giving LR for a non-lc abs. In LR_app's abs case we do case analysis on the LR derivation; if LR_step was used for t1 = abs, Hnv : ~ value t1, but the term is an abs; then ST_AppAbs inversion gives the step's lc premise, hence value (abs) — contradiction. ST_App2 gives value t1 as premise — contradiction. ST_App1 impossible. So the LR_step case works even when t1 happens to be an abs, as long as ~value t1. But in the LR_step case, our proof applies the LR_step rule to app t1 t2 requiring for all reducts... Let me redo: LR_step case gives Hnv : ~ value t1 and Hall. Goal LR B (app t1 t2). Use LR_step (need ~ value (app t1 t2): true since apps are never values... but careful: `value (tm_app ...)` is impossible by inversion of value; prove lemma `value_app_false`. Actually value only has abs/true/false constructors, so `value (tm_app x y)` is impossible by inversion). Then for all t', step (app t1 t2) t' -> LR B t':
  - ST_AppAbs: inversion gives t1 = tm_abs T body. Then the constructor premise locally_closed (tm_abs T body) gives value t1 via v_abs, contradicting Hnv. ✓ (Here lc premise of ST_AppAbs is essential to build value.)
  - ST_App1: t1 --> t1', use Hall + IH_outer.
  - ST_App2: inversion gives value t1, contradicting Hnv.
  ✓ 

So the ~value condition + ST_AppAbs's lc premise is exactly what makes it work. 

Now, what about the LR_abs case in LR_app when the step is ST_App2: the inversion gives value (tm_abs A body) but we ignore it. Fine.

Let me now check the fundamental theorem's abs case without lc:
Goal: LR (A→B) (abs A (body·σ)) where body is the original body variable... Let's call the term t1. Apply LR_abs with body := subst_all σ t1. Need `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open (subst_all σ t1) v)`. Given v, HLRv. Choose x fresh (not in L, not in fvs t1). From typing premise: `has_type (update Γ x A) (open t1 (fvar x)) B`. Apply IH_fundamental to this subderivation with valuation σ' := update_subst σ x v. Need valuation condition: for all y S, (update Γ x A) y = Some S -> LR S (σ' y).
  - y = x case: update Γ x A x = Some A (need Nat.eqb x x = true, then Some A); so S = A and σ' x = v; LR A v ✓.
  - y ≠ x: update Γ x A y = Γ y (need update_neq); σ' y = σ y (Nat.eqb x y = false); then Γ y = Some S and valuation σ gives LR S (σ y) ✓.
Then IH gives LR B (subst_all σ' (open t1 (fvar x))). Rewrite with subst_all_open and freshness x ∉ fvs t1: subst_all σ' t1 = subst_all σ t1, subst_all σ' (fvar x) = v. So LR B (open (subst_all σ t1) v). ✓.

Great. Now the T_Var case and context valuation. We need to define the fundamental theorem:
```
Theorem fundamental : forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T ->
  forall σ, (forall x S, Γ x = Some S -> LR S (σ x)) ->
  LR T (subst_all σ t).
```
Proved by induction on the typing derivation. Note: in the T_Abs case, the IH is for the premise derivation `has_type (update Γ x A) (open t1 (fvar x)) B` for a *specific* x — but the premise in the constructor is a function `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`. In the induction, Coq's generated IH should be:
`forall (x : atom) (Hx : ~ In x L), P (update Γ x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` where P is the statement? Actually the induction principle for has_type with a motive `fun Γ t T => forall σ, valuation σ Γ -> LR T (subst_all σ t)` — the T_Abs constructor has an argument `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Γ x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. The IH generated: `forall x Hx, P (update Γ x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` i.e., `forall x Hx σ, valuation σ (update Γ x T1) -> LR T2 (subst_all σ (open t1 (fvar x)))`. Since the recursive occurrence is under the forall x, ~In x L ->, Coq should generate exactly this IH (with the binders). Good.

Note that in the T_Abs case, the goal is `LR (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (subst_all σ (tm_abs T1 t1))` = `LR (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst_all σ t1))`. Apply LR_abs. Need for all v, LR T1 v -> LR T2 (open (subst_all σ t1) v). Choose x. Get IH x Hx σ'. Note: IH's conclusion is LR T2 (subst_all σ' (open t1 (fvar x))). We rewrite that to LR T2 (open (subst_all σ t1) v). But the goal after LR_abs is LR T2 (open (subst_all σ t1) v) — a definitional equality? `subst_all σ' (open t1 (fvar x))` reduces to `open_rec 0 (subst_all σ' (fvar x)) (subst_all σ' t1)` by the lemma (not definitional). We'll rewrite with the lemma and freshness. Since `open t1 (fvar x)` is `open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1`, and subst_all commutes definitionally? `subst_all σ' (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1)` vs `open_rec 0 (σ' x) (subst_all σ' t1)` — the lemma subst_all_open_rec is proven by induction, so we use `rewrite subst_all_open_rec`. Then σ' x = v (definitional if update_subst uses Nat.eqb x x which reduces? Nat.eqb x x doesn't reduce definitionally for variable x! **LR_step and LR_SN proofs by induction on LR derivation.** Let me think about Coq's induction principle for this inductive:

| LR_true : LR Ty_Bool tm_true
| LR_false : LR Ty_Bool tm_false

Actually, a common pattern:
```
Inductive foo : nat -> Prop :=
| foo_step : forall n, (forall m, m < n -> foo m) -> foo n.
```
The induction principle is `forall P, (forall n, (forall m, m < n -> foo m) -> (forall m, m < n -> P m) -> P n) -> forall n, foo n -> P n`. Yes, generates both the original hypothesis and IH. Coq's guard/positivity handles this. So for LR, the induction principle should include the hypothesis and IHs.

Lemma LR_ind' : forall (P : forall T t, LR T t -> Prop),
  P Ty_Bool tm_true LR_true ->
  P Ty_Bool tm_false LR_false ->
  (forall A B body Hbody,

     (forall t' (Hs : step t t'), P T t' (Hall t' Hs)) ->
     P T t (LR_step T t Hnv Hall)) ->

There's a subtlety with the nested recursive occurrences in LR_abs: the constructor's argument is a function `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)`. When eliminating LR (abs A body) derived via LR_abs, Coq will provide the hypothesis Hbody, and the induction principle needs to provide something for recursive occurrences. Since the occurrence is nested under a function type in a hypothesis, Coq's default scheme may not give IHs at all (it only handles recursive arguments that are direct). In that case, the induction principle might be:
```
LR_ind : forall P,
  P Bool true -> P Bool false ->
  (forall A B body, (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)) -> P (Arrow A B) (abs A body)) ->
  (forall T t, ~value t -> (forall t', step t t' -> LR T t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P T t') -> P T t) ->
  ...
```
Hmm, for LR_step the recursive occurrence is under forall/arrow too (similar to Acc), and Coq does handle Acc specially... Actually Acc's constructor arg is a direct Pi type `forall y, R x y -> Acc R y`; the recursive occurrence appears as the codomain of a function type argument — this is the standard "strictly positive" case and Coq's scheme generation handles it by producing IHs of the form `forall y (H : R x y), P y`. For LR_abs's Hbody, the recursive occurrences are `LR A v` in the *domain* of an arrow inside the hypothesis! That's a negative occurrence position? Let's see: Hbody : forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v). In the type of the constructor, Hbody is an argument of type (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)). Recursive occurrences: LR A v appears to the left of an arrow (negative position), and LR B (...) to the right (positive). For strict positivity of the inductive definition, occurrences in the arguments of the constructor must be strictly positive. Is `(forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v))` a strictly positive occurrence? The type is an arrow: domain = LR A v (contains LR negatively), codomain = LR B (positive). In a constructor argument type, an occurrence in the domain of an arrow is negative... Wait, strict positivity checking: for constructor `c : T1 -> T2 -> I`, each Ti must be "strictly positive" in the sense that I does not occur in negative positions. `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (...)` as a type is `forall v, (LR A v -> LR B ...)`. Inside, LR A v is in the domain of the inner arrow, i.e., to the left of an odd number of arrows: negative occurrence! Is that allowed?

Coq's positivity checker: nested inductive types require strict positivity. An occurrence of I in the argument of a constructor must be strictly positive. In `forall v, A -> B`, the occurrence of I in A is in a negative position. Coq rejects nested occurrences in negative positions generally. Hmm! For example:
```
Inductive bad : Type :=
| bad_intro : (bad -> nat) -> bad.
```
is rejected ("non strictly positive occurrence"). Our LR_abs with `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)`: the occurrence LR A v is in a negative position, so Coq will reject this inductive definition as non-strictly-positive! Indeed, this is the classic problem with logical relations: contravariance. The standard solution is to define the relation by recursion on the type (Fixpoint), not inductively, because arrow types have negative occurrences.

Hmm. So the inductive LR with `forall v, LR A v -> LR B (open body v)` won't be accepted. Damn.

Wait, but can we restructure to make LR not depend negatively? Standard "reducibility candidate" definitions in Coq are usually done by Fixpoint on the type, not inductive. The inductive approach works for "hereditarily terminating" definitions where recursion is positive, e.g., `Inductive SN : tm -> Prop`. But logical relations with function types are contravariant.

Hmm. So we need the Fixpoint LR definition. Back to the drawing board, but maybe we can incorporate the "step" rule idea using a Fixpoint with an extra parameter? Let's reconsider.

Fixpoint LR T t := match T with
  | Bool => ...
  | Arrow A B => ...
end.
With LR A appearing negatively in the Arrow case, but that's fine for Fixpoint on T (structural recursion on the type). So we can define:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow A B => SN t /\ (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
and prove everything by induction on types. This was our earlier definition sans lc. But we hit the T_If-at-arrow-type issue and the app closure issues. Let's revisit with the ideas from the inductive attempt, to see if the Fixpoint definition can be handled with the "LR_step/expansion" lemmas.

With Fixpoint LR:
```
LR Bool t := SN t
LR (A→B) t := SN t /\ forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app t v)
```
Properties to prove by induction on the type T:
1. `LR_SN : LR T t -> SN t` — immediate by definition (both cases include SN). No induction needed.
2. `LR_step : LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'` — by induction on T.
   - Bool: SN t' from SN t.
   - Arrow A B: need SN t' ✓ and forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app t' v). We have LR B (app t v). Need step (app t v) (app t' v) to apply IH_B. Constructing this step requires ST_App1 with premise locally_closed v, i.e., ST_App1 : step t t' -> locally_closed v -> step (app t v) (app t' v). So we need LR A v -> locally_closed v. If LR doesn't include lc, we need a separate lemma `LR_lc : LR T t -> locally_closed t`, proved by induction on T:
     - Bool: SN t doesn't give lc! Counterexample: t = tm_bvar 0 is SN but not lc. Hmm. So LR_lc is false with LR Bool := SN. We could add lc to LR Bool: `LR Bool t := locally_closed t /\ SN t`. Then LR_lc holds by definition for Bool; for Arrow A B: LR includes lc explicitly.
     - Then LR_step at Arrow needs lc v (from LR A v) ✓ to construct ST_App1, then IH_B gives LR B (app t' v). But IH_B : LR B s -> step s s' -> LR B s' — we need a hypothesis step (app t v) (app t' v) ✓.
     So define LR with lc in both cases:
     ```
     LR Bool t := locally_closed t /\ SN t
     LR (A→B) t := locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app t v))
     ```
     Then LR_step by induction on T works.
3. LR_lc by induction on T: Bool lc from conjunct; Arrow lc from conjunct. ✓.
4. Now the fundamental theorem. But we still have the T_If at arrow type problem: need application closure of if-terms. Hmm, let's revisit with the new "step-based" ideas.

Maybe we can prove a general compatibility lemma for if at any type T by induction on T, using LR_step:
`LR_if : forall t1 T t2 t3, LR Bool t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> LR T (if t1 t2 t3)`.
Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: need lc (if...) and SN (if...). lc from LR_lc of components. SN: prove by induction on SN t1 as earlier (using the given steps of the if-term). Actually simpler: SN (if t1 t2 t3) follows from SN t1, SN t2, SN t3 by the SN_if lemma (proven by SN_ind on t1). ✓.
- Arrow A B: need lc (if ...) ✓; SN (if ...) ✓ (SN_if); and forall v, LR A v -> LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v).

  `LR_app_if : forall t1 t2 t3 v A B, LR Bool t1 -> LR (A→B) t2 -> LR (A→B) t3 -> LR A v -> LR B (app (if t1 t2 t3) v)`? Hmm, we can't apply IH on B directly since the term isn't an if of type B (it's an app).
  But we can use LR_step/expansion: app (if t1 t2 t3) v. Since SN (if ...) and SN v, can we prove LR B of the app by considering its reducts? The reducts:
   - if the if-term steps via ST_IfTrue to t2, then app (if...) v --> app t2 v (needs ST_App1 with lc v) and LR B (app t2 v) from LR (A→B) t2's app closure.
   - similarly t3.

   - if the if-term is stuck, app is stuck (App1 needs if --> ; App2 needs value of if — if is not a value) → SN vacuous, but LR B includes the app closure conjunct... If app X v is stuck (X stuck), then app (app X v) w doesn't step, so SN and its app closure... this could go on forever structurally? The Fixpoint definition is structurally recursive on type, so for a term like app X v : B = C→D, we need to show LR (app (app X v) w) at type D, requiring app closure again. There's no obvious base. But note: if the term is stuck, all its applications are stuck, so SN holds, but LR at arrow requires application closure all the way down the type; each step reduces type, so eventually base type Bool and we need SN (stuck) ✓. So the proof can be by induction on the type B: for B = Bool, SN is enough; for B = arrow, need closure under one more application, using induction.

Hold on, this suggests a general lemma: if SN t, and t is "stuck-ish"/has certain reducts... Could we avoid needing LR (B1→B2) (app t1 t2) by proving SN/closure directly for the iterated application? Let's reconsider the "multi-step to value" definition of LR, which handles this more gracefully because E/T relations are defined via multi-step:

```
LR T t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', t -->* t' -> value t' -> VR T t')
VR Bool v := v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false
VR (A→B) v := forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app v w)
```
This is a Fixpoint on T (VR at arrow references LR at A and B; LR references VR at T; structural on T? `LR T` calls `VR T`; `VR (A→B)` calls `LR A` and `LR B`. If we define them as a mutual Fixpoint on T, Coq accepts structural recursion: LR T uses VR T (same type? Hmm, VR T is defined by matching on T; LR T calls VR T on the same T. Mutual recursion where the call is on the same T might be rejected unless it's a nested fixpoint with the same decreasing argument... We can define a single Fixpoint returning a pair/and:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => fun t => lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> (t' = true \/ t' = false))
  | Arrow A B => fun t => lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> (forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app t' w)))
  end.
```
Here `LR (Arrow A B) t` uses `LR A` and `LR B` — structural. And the VR condition is inlined with multi-step and value. This is a Fixpoint on T. 

Now let's revisit the properties:
- LR_lc: ✓ by definition (lc conjunct in both cases).
- LR_SN: ✓.
- LR_step: LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'. Proof by induction on T:
  - Bool: lc t'? Need step preserves lc! Hmm, LR Bool t includes lc t; to prove lc t' we need lc preserved forward by step. Is that true? Let's check ST_AppAbs: app (abs T body) v --> open body v. If app is lc, then abs is lc (lc_at 1 body) and v is lc; open body v: need lc. Opening an lc_at 1 term with an lc term gives lc: lc_at_open_rec with k=0: lc_at 1 body, lc_at 0 v → lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v body) ✓. ST_App1: app t1 t2 lc → t1,t2 lc; t1 --> t1' — is t1' lc? By induction on the step derivation, presumably. ST_App2: hmm. ST_IfTrue: if t1 t2 t3 lc → t2 lc ✓. ST_If: t1 --> t1', need if t1' t2 t3 lc from t1', t2, t3 ✓. So `step_lc : step t t' -> lc t -> lc t'` provable by induction on step. Note ST_AppAbs uses lc_at_open_rec. ✓ This is a standard lemma. Good.
    Then LR_step at Bool: lc t' ✓; SN t' from SN t; multi-reduct condition: t' -->* t'' value → t -->* t'' via step then multi ✓.
  - Arrow A B: lc t' ✓ (step_lc); SN t' ✓ (SN_step); forall t'' multi t' t'' value t'', forall w LR A w -> LR B (app t'' w): from LR T t's condition: t -->* t'' via step/multi, then app t'' w ✓. No induction on type needed! Great, LR_step is easy with this definition (just step_lc and SN_step).
  
- Expansion/backward: not needed maybe.
- LR_app: LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2).
  Let's see. LR B (app t1 t2) requires:
  1. lc(app) ✓.
  2. SN(app t1 t2): need to prove. 
  3. Multi-reduct condition: forall s, app t1 t2 -->* s -> value s -> (if B arrow, forall w, LR B1 w -> LR B2 (app s w); if B Bool, s = true/false).
  
  Hmm, (3) for B arrow requires app s w for a *value* s reachable from app t1 t2. If s is a value, s must be an abs. Then app s w's behavior... This is similar complexity.

Let's tackle SN(app t1 t2) with this definition: 
`LR (A→B) t1` gives lc, SN, and the multi-value condition: every value reachable from t1 is an abs v with forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app v w).
To prove SN(app t1 t2), use SN_intro and take a step app t1 t2 --> s:
  - ST_AppAbs: t1 = abs body, t2 = v value, s = open body v. Need SN(open body v). From LR(A→B) t1's value condition with t1 as a value reachable by multi_refl: VR gives forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app (abs body) w). With w := v (LR A v), LR B (app (abs body) v) → SN(app (abs body) v).   - ST_App2: value t1, t2 --> t2', reduc app t1 t2'. Need SN(app t1 t2'). Here t1 is a value. Since value t1 and LR (A→B) t1, the value condition gives VR: forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app t1 w). For t2' with LR A t2', we get LR B (app t1 t2') → SN. But LR A t2' from LR_step A with step t2 t2' ✓. So no inner induction needed! Because t1 is a value and we can use the value condition directly on t2'.
  
  So SN_app_LR: by outer SN_ind on t1 with P t1 := LR (A→B) t1 -> forall t2, LR A t2 -> SN (app t1 t2). In ST_App2 we don't need inner IH because t1 is a value and LR's value condition applies to the *same* t1 (not a reduct). Wait, but the value condition is about t1 reachable via multi_refl: VR(A→B) t1 requires value t1 ✓ in ST_App2. Then for w := t2', LR B (app t1 t2') → SN. ✓. In ST_App1, IH on t1' with LR_step. ✓. In ST_AppAbs, use value condition of t1 (which equals abs body, a value?   Great, SN_app_LR works out nicely with this definition.

Now (3): the multi-reduct/value condition for LR B (app t1 t2). Let's think:
  For all s with app t1 t2 -->* s and value s, need VR B s (where VR Bool s := s = true \/ false; VR (B1→B2) s := forall w, LR B1 w -> LR B2 (app s w)).
  
  Hmm. If B = Bool: s is a value reachable from app t1 t2. Values of Bool type are true/false; could app t1 t2 reduce to true? Typing-wise no, but semantically our LR doesn't track types... Wait, if t1 ∈ LR (A→B) with B=Bool and t2 ∈ LR A, then semantically app t1 t2 should be a Bool-typed term; can it reduce to an abs? In an untyped sense maybe not, but LR doesn't enforce that t1's value condition only allows abs. Let's look: LR (A→B) t1's multi-value condition VR(A→B) v for each value v reachable: it's `forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app v w)`. If v = true (a value), VR(A→B) true = forall w, LR A w -> LR Bool (app true w). Is that ever satisfied? LR Bool (app true w) requires SN(app true w) and the multi-value condition: app true w reduces (via ST_App2) as w reduces; if w ∈ LR A then w is SN and reaches a value; when w is a value, app true w is stuck. So SN holds. Value-reducts of app true w: during the reduction, app true w' with w' a value — app true w' is not a value. So no value reducts; the condition is vacuous. So VR(A→B) true can hold! Thus LR (A→B) true can hold (if lc, SN, and this condition). Interesting.
  
  So for the result: if app t1 t2 -->* s with s a value, could s be true? E.g., t1 = true, t2 something: app true t2 never reduces to a value (it only reduces in t2 and stays an app). t1 = abs body: app (abs body) v reduces to open body v when v value, which could then reduce to true. So yes, s could be true. Then for B = Bool, VR Bool s requires s = true or false ✓ — it is! Wait, we need to know s is true or false; s is a value. Values include abs. Could app t1 t2 reduce to an abs while B = Bool? If t1 = abs body and body evaluates to an abs, then open body v reduces to an abs; but semantically body has type B = Bool, so LR B (open body v) would prevent it from being an abs? Hmm, LR B for B = Bool requires: multi-value condition: if open body v -->* abs, then VR Bool (abs) requires abs = true/false — impossible. So if LR B (open body v) holds, then no abs reduct exists. But we need LR B (open body v) — do we have it? In the ST_AppAbs case, the value condition of t1 gives LR B (app (abs) v), not LR B (open body v). Hmm! To get the value condition for the result s, we need to track reductions through beta.

This is the fundamental difficulty: LR B of `app (abs body) v` (from the function's value condition) vs. LR B of its reduct `open body v`. Both should be equivalent if step is deterministic and LR is closed under steps AND expansion. Expansion is the key missing lemma.

So let's try to prove expansion for this LR definition:
`LR_expand : forall T t t', lc t -> step t t' -> LR T t' -> LR T t`.
By induction on T:
- Bool: lc ✓; SN t: all reducts of t are... we need SN t from SN t' and step t t'. Since step is deterministic, and t has a reduct t' which is SN; other reducts are all equal t' (determinism), so SN t. Actually, we can prove SN t directly: SN_intro with Hall t'' Hstep: determinism gives t'' = t', so SN t'' from SN t'. Need step_det. ✓.
  Multi-value condition: for all s, t -->* s -> value s -> s = true\/false. Given multi t s. If t = s (refl), then t is a value and step t t' contradicts value_no_step. Otherwise multi_step t u s with step t u; determinism gives u = t'; then t' -->* s; LR T t' gives s canonical ✓.
  Alternatively: we can prove a general "multi_step prepend" lemma: step t t' -> multi t' s -> multi t s. ✓.
- Arrow A B: lc ✓; SN t ✓ same; value condition: forall s, t -->* s -> value s -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app s w). Same argument: if t -->* s via refl then t value and steps contradiction; else t --> t' -->* s (determinism), use LR T t' value condition: VR gives forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app s w) ✓. 
  Wait, this is much simpler than I thought! We don't need application closure at all for expansion? Let's double check: LR (A→B) t' gives the condition: forall s, t' -->* s -> value s -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app s w). We need the same for t. Using determinism: any s reachable from t is reachable from t' (since t --> t' is the unique first step; if t -->* s is refl then s = t; but t is a value? No—t steps, so t is not a value (values don't step); hence s = t is not a value, so the value premise fails). Wait, "multi t s" with refl gives s = t; value s = value t; but step t t' and value t would mean values step, contradiction. ✓.   So expansion at Arrow doesn't need T induction at all! The VR condition is about *values* reachable; and reductions after t' are handled by LR t'. 

So with the multi-value formulation, both LR_step and LR_expand are straightforward (given determinism and value_no_step). And the LR_app lemma might become easier. Let's redo LR_app fully:

So the plan works! Let me also double-check the value condition in LR_step: LR_step at type B uses `step_lc` (to preserve lc). We need `step_lc : step t t' -> locally_closed t -> locally_closed t'`. Let's verify this is provable:
- ST_AppAbs: app (abs T t) v --> open t v. lc(app (abs T t) v) = lc_at 0 app ... means lc_at 0 (abs T t) — i.e., lc_at 1 t — and lc_at 0 v. Need lc_at 0 (open t v) = lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v t). Because t's bvars are < k+1: those < k remain; bvar k replaced by u; and under abstractions, k increases consistently. Let's prove:
  ```
  Lemma lc_at_open_rec_S : forall k u t, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
  ```
  Induction on t generalizing k:
  - bvar i: open_rec k u (bvar i) = if Nat.eqb k i then u else bvar i. lc_at (S k) (bvar i) gives i < S k. If k = i: u, lc_at k u ✓. Else bvar i: need i < k. From i < S k and i ≠ k, get i < k (Nat.lt_neq? use lia). ✓.
  - abs T t1: open_rec k u (abs T t1) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u t1). lc_at (S k) (abs T t1) → lc_at (S (S k)) t1. IH with k := S k: lc_at (S (S k)) t1 -> lc_at (S k) u -> lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u t1). Need lc_at (S k) u from lc_at k u via monotonicity ✓. Then lc_abs gives lc_at k (abs ...) ✓.
  - app/if/true/false: straightforward.
  ```
  Then ST_AppAbs's lc goal: lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v t) from lc_at 1 t and lc_at 0 v ✓ (k=0).
- ST_App1: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2, lc app → lc t1, lc t2; IH on step t1 t1' with lc t1 gives lc t1'; then lc app ✓.

Now let's re-examine LR_step more carefully with the final definition:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t =>
      locally_closed t /\
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> t' = tm_true \/ t' = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow A B => fun t =>
      locally_closed t /\
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (tm_app t' w))
  end.
```
Wait, in the Arrow case, the value condition is `forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app t' w)`. This matches VR(A→B) v := forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app v w). Good.

Note the structural recursion: `LR (Ty_Arrow A B)` calls `LR A` and `LR B`. Coq accepts (A and B are subterms). But the recursive calls appear inside a `fun w => ...` inside... The Fixpoint's decreasing argument is T; all recursive calls are on A or B, structurally smaller. Coq should accept. The definition might need to be written as a Fixpoint with match returning a function; Coq handles this.

Actually, there's a subtlety: `LR A w` appears in a negative position? For a Fixpoint, negative positions don't matter (it's not an inductive type). Coq only checks structural recursion. ✓.

Now let's verify LR_step (forward closure) with this definition:
`Lemma LR_step : forall T t t', LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'.`
Proof by induction on T? Or can we prove it uniformly? Let's see:
- Unfold LR T t. It gives lc t, SN t, and the value condition.
- Need lc t': step_lc t t' Hlc.
- SN t': SN_step.
- Value condition for t': 
  * Bool: forall s, t' -->* s -> value s -> s = true \/ false. Given multi t' s; multi t s via multi_step (step t t') multi t' s; original condition gives ✓.
  * Arrow: forall s, t' -->* s -> value s -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app s w). Same: multi t s via multi_step; original ✓.
So LR_step is proven uniformly without induction on T, as long as `LR T t` can be unfolded/destructed. Since T is a variable, `destruct (LR T t)` — can we destruct an unfolded Fixpoint when T is a variable? `LR` is a Fixpoint; `unfold LR in H` won't reduce when T is a variable (match stuck). Hmm! We need to case on T: `destruct T`. Then LR reduces. So proof: `intros T; destruct T; ...` Actually we can define a helper lemma:
```
Lemma LR_inv : forall T t, LR T t ->
  locally_closed t /\ SN t /\
  match T with
  | Bool => forall s, multi step t s -> value s -> s = true \/ s = false
  | Arrow A B => forall s, multi step t s -> value s -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app s w)
  end.
Proof. intros T; destruct T; simpl; tauto. Qed.
```
Hmm, `simpl` on LR (Ty_Bool) t should reduce. Let me think about how to structure this. The Fixpoint `LR` with a match on T returning a function: `LR Ty_Bool` should definitionally reduce to the Bool branch function. So `destruct T; simpl; intros H; exact H` etc.

Alternatively, define LR via a definition with explicit match:
```
Definition LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ SN t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> t' = tm_true \/ t' = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (tm_app t' w)
  end.
```
But this is not structurally recursive as written (LR appears in its own body) — Coq rejects unless it's a Fixpoint. We can use a Fixpoint with a helper:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ SN t /\
  match T with ... end.
```
where the recursive calls are on A and B. Coq's guard checker: recursive calls `LR A w`, `LR B (tm_app t' w)`; the decreasing argument is T = Ty_Arrow A B; A and B are subterms. Should pass. But note the match on T is inside the body; the recursive calls appear in the Arrow branch — okay.

Hmm, one issue: `Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop := ...` — the recursive call `LR A w` has A : ty, w : tm; fine. But the guard checker requires the recursive calls to be on structurally smaller arguments; A, B are direct subterms of the matched T. ✓.

Let's now write the properties:

```
Definition LR_bool_cond t := forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> t' = tm_true \/ t' = tm_false.
Definition LR_arrow_cond A B t := forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall w, LR A w -> LR B (app t' w).
```
Maybe simpler to prove lemmas by `destruct T` and `simpl in *`.

Let me now also check the **fundamental theorem's app case with the multi-value LR**:
Goal LR B (app (t1σ) (t2σ)) given LR (A→B) (t1σ) and LR A (t2σ). We proved LR_app above. Let me re-verify the proof structure with all the pieces:

```
Lemma LR_app_value : forall A B t1,
  value t1 -> LR (A→B) t1 ->
  forall t2, SN t2 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros A B t1 Hval H1 t2 Hsn2.
  induction Hsn2 as [t2 Hstep2 IH2].  (* motive includes HLR2? *)
  intros HLR2.
  ...
```
Hmm, the motive for SN_ind on t2 should be `fun t2 => LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2)`. When we `induction Hsn2`, Coq generalizes t2 in the goal. If the goal is `LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2)`, and Hsn2 : SN t2, then `induction Hsn2` gives motive `fun t => LR A t -> LR B (app t1 t)` with IH `forall t', step t2 t' -> LR A t' -> LR B (app t1 t')` (assuming Coq's scheme handles the nested forall). Good.

To prove `LR A t2 -> LR B (app t1 t2)`:
  introduce HLR2. We need to establish the three conjuncts of LR B (app t1 t2). But B is a variable; we need to destruct B to unfold LR B. So:
```
  destruct B as [| B1 B2].
  - (* B = Bool *)
    repeat split? 
```
Actually the LR definition as a Fixpoint: for `LR Ty_Bool (app t1 t2)`, simpl gives `lc (app ...) /\ SN (app ...) /\ cond`. To prove a conjunction, `split` etc. Let me structure with helper constructor-ish lemmas:

LR T t calls value_cond T s — same T, not smaller! In a mutual Fixpoint, calls must be on a structurally smaller argument, except the principal call structure. Mutual recursion with the same argument T is allowed if it's a "nested" call? No: in mutual fixpoints, you can call the other function on the same or smaller argument? Coq's guard condition: mutual recursive calls must have a decreasing argument; calling `value_cond T` from `LR T` with the same T is allowed in some cases? I believe Coq allows mutual calls on the same argument (the "principal argument" relation requires that in each call, either the argument decreases or stays the same while moving to another function in the block?). Actually, Coq's guard checker allows calls between mutually defined functions with the same decreasing argument, as long as the recursive argument is a variable in the same "recursive position".
